<a id='question-150'></a>

### Question 150: Acute Adrenal Crisis in Septic Shock
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Management of acute adrenal crisis (stress-dose corticosteroids) in the context of septic shock, considering underlying chronic steroid use and potential drug interactions. (应对慢性类固醇使用背景下的脓毒症休克中的急性肾上腺危象管理，考虑药物相互作用)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 64-year-old female with a long-standing history of severe rheumatoid arthritis (RA) presents to the emergency department with acute onset abdominal pain, fever (38.5°C), tachycardia (120 bpm), hypotension (BP 85/50 mmHg), and altered mental status. Her chronic medications include prednisone 20 mg daily, methotrexate 20 mg weekly, and naproxen 1000 mg twice daily. Physical examination reveals a rigid abdomen with rebound tenderness and guarding. Laboratory results show leukocytosis (WBC 25,000/µL with left shift), elevated lactate (6 mmol/L), and acute kidney injury (BUN 40 mg/dL, creatinine 2.5 mg/dL). An abdominal CT scan confirms a perforated duodenal ulcer with free air and peritonitis. The patient is diagnosed with septic shock secondary to perforated viscus. Given her chronic prednisone use, what is the MOST appropriate immediate pharmacological intervention to prevent a potentially fatal complication?

(A) Administer intravenous ranitidine to reduce gastric acid secretion and promote ulcer healing.
(B) Administer intravenous metoprolol to control the sinus tachycardia associated with sepsis and reduce myocardial oxygen demand.
(C) Administer intravenous hydrocortisone 100 mg every 8 hours.
(D) Administer intravenous omeprazole to suppress gastric acid production and prevent further ulceration.
(E) Administer intravenous vancomycin to cover potential methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus (MRSA) infection.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Pharmacological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 64yo female with severe RA. This suggests chronic inflammation and likely chronic immunosuppression/steroid use.
- **Chronic Medications:** Prednisone 20 mg daily (significant chronic steroid use), Methotrexate 20 mg weekly (immunosuppressant), Naproxen 1000 mg BID (high-dose NSAID).
- **Acute Presentation:** Septic shock (hypotension, tachycardia, altered mental status, leukocytosis, high lactate) secondary to perforated duodenal ulcer peritonitis.
- **Key Clue:** Chronic prednisone use (20 mg/day is considered significant, suppressing the HPA axis). Patients on chronic steroids >5mg/day for >3 weeks have adrenal suppression.
- **Clinical Context:** Septic shock is a major physiological stressor. Stress triggers the HPA axis to release cortisol. In patients with adrenal suppression, the adrenal glands cannot mount an adequate cortisol response to stress.
- **Potential Complication:** Acute adrenal crisis (adrenal insufficiency) superimposed on septic shock, which can lead to refractory hypotension, cardiovascular collapse, and death.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST appropriate immediate pharmacological intervention to prevent a potentially fatal complication.

##### 2. Pharmacological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生化、靶点受体与机制深度剖析)
- **Pathophysiology of Adrenal Suppression:** Chronic glucocorticoid (prednisone) administration suppresses the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis. The hypothalamus reduces CRH release, the pituitary reduces ACTH release, and the adrenal cortex reduces cortisol production.
- **Stress Response:** Major physiological stressors like sepsis, trauma, or surgery stimulate the HPA axis, leading to increased cortisol release. Cortisol is crucial for maintaining vascular tone (via potentiation of catecholamines on alpha-1 receptors), glucose metabolism, and immune modulation during stress.
- **Adrenal Crisis in Stress:** In patients with adrenal suppression, the adrenal glands cannot produce sufficient cortisol during stress. This leads to relative glucocorticoid deficiency, causing hypotension refractory to vasopressors, hypoglycemia, and potentially cardiovascular collapse.
- **Treatment of Adrenal Crisis:** The immediate treatment is stress-dose glucocorticoids, typically intravenous hydrocortisone (100 mg IV every 8 hours) because it has both glucocorticoid and mineralocorticoid activity. This replaces the deficient cortisol, supports vascular tone, and helps stabilize the patient.
- **Why Hydrocortisone?** Hydrocortisone is preferred over other glucocorticoids like dexamethasone or methylprednisolone in adrenal crisis because it has mineralocorticoid activity (mimicking aldosterone), which helps maintain sodium and water balance and blood pressure. Dexamethasone and methylprednisolone have minimal mineralocorticoid activity.
- **Relevance to Sepsis:** Guidelines recommend stress-dose corticosteroids for patients with septic shock who have evidence of adrenal insufficiency or who are refractory to fluid resuscitation and vasopressors. Given the patient's chronic prednisone use, she is at high risk for adrenal insufficiency during this septic shock episode.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Administer intravenous ranitidine:** Ranitidine is an H2 receptor antagonist used to reduce gastric acid secretion. While reducing acid might theoretically help ulcer healing, it does not address the immediate life-threatening risk of adrenal crisis in a patient on chronic steroids experiencing septic shock. This is not the priority intervention.
- (B) **Administer intravenous metoprolol:** Metoprolol is a selective beta-1 blocker. While the patient has tachycardia (120 bpm), this is likely compensatory in the setting of septic shock and hypotension. Beta-blockers are generally contraindicated in septic shock as they can worsen hypotension and impair the compensatory response. Furthermore, this patient has a history of severe asthma, making beta-blockers particularly risky due to the potential for bronchospasm, even with selective agents. This intervention is inappropriate and potentially harmful.
- (D) **Administer intravenous omeprazole:** Omeprazole is a proton pump inhibitor (PPI) used to suppress gastric acid production. Like ranitidine, it addresses the underlying ulcer but not the immediate life-threatening risk of adrenal crisis. PPIs take time to exert their full effect and are not the priority in acute septic shock with suspected adrenal insufficiency.
- (E) **Administer intravenous vancomycin:** Vancomycin is an antibiotic used primarily against Gram-positive bacteria, including MRSA. While broad-spectrum antibiotics are crucial in septic shock, the choice of vancomycin depends on the suspected source and potential pathogens. Perforated duodenal ulcers are typically caused by Gram-negative bacteria (e.g., E. coli, Klebsiella) or sometimes anaerobes. While MRSA is possible, empiric coverage usually starts with agents like piperacillin-tazobactam or a carbapenem (meropenem) for polymicrobial intra-abdominal infection. More importantly, this option does not address the specific, potentially fatal complication related to the patient's chronic steroid use (adrenal crisis).

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Patients on chronic glucocorticoid therapy (>5mg prednisone equivalent for >3 weeks) are at risk for adrenal suppression. During major physiological stress (like sepsis, surgery, trauma), these patients require stress-dose glucocorticoids (e.g., IV hydrocortisone) to prevent acute adrenal crisis, which can manifest as refractory hypotension.
- **Differentials:** This question integrates pharmacology (glucocorticoid effects, HPA axis suppression), pathophysiology (septic shock, adrenal crisis), and clinical management. Key differentials include recognizing the signs of adrenal crisis versus other causes of refractory shock, understanding the role of stress-dose steroids in critically ill patients, and differentiating appropriate interventions for septic shock versus ulcer management. Also, consider the contraindications of beta-blockers in asthma and septic shock.
